## Page 5

City of Minneapolis: 2016 Resident Survey 
1 
Wilder Research, December 2016 
Data collection 
Survey instrument design 
Staff from Wilder Research and the City of Minneapolis Neighborhood and Community Relations 
Department (NCR) developed the survey in 2016. In developing a new survey instrument rather 
than using the survey that the City had used in previous years, there were two primary goals: 
one, to reduce the length of the survey from previous years; two, to create a survey that was 
more specific to and useful for the City of Minneapolis in its present and future work. Existing 
items from the 2012 Resident Survey were used to design some of the items on the survey 
instrument to produce trend data for important City goals.  
As part of the survey development process, Wilder Research and NCR staff met with department 
heads across City government to further refine the survey and ensure that each question would 
be useful for measuring progress toward City goals. The survey instrument was also reviewed 
for readability and bilingual Wilder staff who speak Spanish, Somali, and Hmong completed a 
“cultural review” of the instrument to be sure all of the concepts would make sense and would 
not be offensive or inappropriate for people from their cultural communities.  
The survey was formatted by Wilder Research as an English language survey that residents could 
fill out themselves on paper. Residents were given the option to take the survey in one of three 
ways: online by filling out a confidential web survey; via a phone call with Wilder Research in 
English, Hmong, Somali, or Spanish; or by filling out a hard copy and mailing it in via the enclosed 
postage paid return envelope.  Completed paper surveys were processed by Wilder and entered 
into the Web survey template for analysis. 
Sample 
The sample was stratified by the City’s 11 residential communities. To obtain a statistically 
representative sample of adults for each of the 11 communities, a random sample of 1,000 
residential addresses in each community was purchased from a national sampling vendor 
(Marketing Systems Group of Horsham, PA). Address-based sampling was used so that all 
households would have an equal chance of being sampled for the survey, regardless of their 
phone status. Marketing Systems Group (MSG) obtained the list of addresses from the U.S. 
Postal Service.  
Pre-test 
In April 2016, before the full survey administration began, a pre-test was conducted to make 
sure the survey was not too long or difficult to fill out and that residents understood what was

## Page 6

City of Minneapolis: 2016 Resident Survey 
2 
Wilder Research, December 2016 
being asked. According to the results from the pre-test, the survey instrument was working 
properly, had no major problem areas. Therefore, no major changes were made to the survey 
after the pre-test and prior to fielding the full data collection process. 
The pre-test was also used to determine which mode (postcard, note card, or mailed survey 
packet) would provide the most responses. This was done to plan the best mailing order for the 
full survey administration. The pre-test was sent to a total of 550 addresses, split evenly by 
residential community (50 invitations sent per community) and by mode (postcard, note card, and 
survey packet). Figure 1 below provides an overview of completed surveys by mode and method 
of completion (surveys completed on the Web vs. on paper). Based on the response to the pre-
test, the note card was sent first, followed by a reminder postcard, then a full survey packet.

Data collection protocol 
For the full survey, Wilder Research used the following method of survey mailing: an initial note 
card in a brightly colored envelope was mailed on July 6, 2016 to 11,000 sampled households. 
The note card included a brief explanation of the survey and a Web link for residents to 
complete the survey online. Residents were also given the option to call Wilder Research to 
complete the survey over the phone in English, Hmong, Somali, and Spanish. Seven days after 
the note cards were mailed, a postcard was sent to all sampled households, reminding those 
who had not yet completed the survey online to do so and thanking those who had already 
responded. Approximately two weeks after the reminder postcards were mailed, a full survey 
packet was mailed to all households that still had not completed a survey. The survey packet 
included a cover letter, the survey itself, and a postage-paid return envelope. The survey packet 
also included a Web link to complete the survey online. The remaining completed surveys were 
received over the next 7 weeks, with the final date for survey receipt being September 9, 2016. 
All note cards, postcards, and survey packets were addressed to “Minneapolis Resident.”

City of Minneapolis: 2016 Resident Survey

Wilder Research, December 2016 
Outreach 
Wilder Research completed additional outreach in communities and among demographic 
groups that had completed surveys at a lower rate. One month before the end of the survey 
period, Wilder Research staff called phone numbers (both cell phones and landlines) that were 
connected to sampled addresses in three communities with fewer completed surveys: Near 
North, Phillips, and University. These calls were made to encourage residents to complete the 
survey if they had not already done so. In the final two weeks of the survey period, Wilder Research 
staff also went in person to sampled addresses in the Phillips community for additional 
outreach. For all of this outreach, we focused on addresses that were linked to surnames that 
indicated these residents may be Somali, Latinx, or Hmong. Bilingual Wilder staff completed 
much of this outreach, to promote additional access to the survey for respondents for whom 
English is not their first language.   
Completed surveys 
Completed surveys were received from 2,320 residents of the 11 sampling communities. The 
overall response rate was 22 percent (2,320 of 10,458 eligible surveys). Community-specific 
population, sample, and response rates can be found in Figure 2 below.

## Page 9

City of Minneapolis: 2016 Resident Survey 
5 
Wilder Research, December 2016 
Data cleaning and analysis 
Wilder Research checked all completed paper surveys as they were returned. After the paper 
surveys were entered and the paper and web survey data were combined, the survey data were 
weighted to create reliable estimates of population characteristics for each of the 12 sampling 
areas: the 11 community districts and the City of Minneapolis as a whole. Weighting also 
compensates for practical limitations of a sample survey; for example, some demographic 
groups are less likely to respond to surveys. Weighting reduces the bias of survey estimates by 
using demographic information about the population being surveyed, in this case the population 
of the City of Minneapolis and its 11 residential communities.  
The weighting process entailed three steps. In the first step, base weights were calculated to 
reflect the number of adults in each sampled household. In the second step, those base weights 
were adjusted so that the resulting weights totaled existing data on the total population, based 
on age, gender, race and ethnicity, housing tenure (home owners vs. renters), and geographic 
location (planning district). For this study, survey weights were calculated using the method of 
Iterative Proportional Fitting, which is commonly referred to as Raking. In some cases, some 
values for demographic questions were missing or incomplete. Missing values were replaced, 
where possible, by logical inference. A hot-deck imputation procedure was used to assign the 
remaining missing values. This resulted in 2,320 cases that were eligible to be weighted. The 
data were weighted against the U.S. Census Bureau’s 2010-2014 estimates from the American 
Community Survey: http://factfinder2.census.gov/. In the final step, the adjusted weights were 
put through a series of quality control checks to detect extreme outliers (to make sure no one 
respondent counts too strongly in the analysis) and to prevent any errors in calculations or 
procedures. 
Once the data were weighted, the tables in the data book were created to show differences in 
survey responses across different groups of residents (see the section below for more details). 
Wilder Research used these tables to write the main report by looking for notable differences 
between groups and the overall citywide result. Wilder defined a notable difference as one of  
10 percentage points or more.

## Page 10

City of Minneapolis: 2016 Resident Survey 
6 
Wilder Research, December 2016 
How to use this data book 
Each table in this data book represents a question from the survey. For every table in this data 
book, results are shown for groups of residents from the City of Minneapolis based on: 
 Residential community: Calhoun Isle, Camden, Central, Longfellow, Near North, Nokomis, 
Northeast, Phillips, Powderhorn, Southwest, and University 
 Gender: female, male1 
 Age: adults age 18-34, 35-44, 45-54, 55-64, and 65 and older 
 Race and ethnicity: African American or African-born; American Indian or Native American; 
Asian or Asian American; Hispanic, Latino, or Spanish origin (of any race); and White (non-
Hispanic) 
 Annual household income: less than $15,000; $15,000 to $34,999;  $35,000 to $49,999; 
$50,000 to $99,999; $100,000 to $149,999; and $150,000 or more 
 Housing tenure: renting, owning 
The columns in each table represent the response options for each survey question. (See the 
Appendix for the survey.) For survey questions where residents chose from a pre-determined 
list of responses, each column shows the percentage of residents who gave each possible 
response to the question. Some response categories are combined because of the low number 
of responses in those categories. Some questions were open-ended, meaning that respondents 
wrote in their own answers instead of choosing from a pre-determined list. For these questions, 
answers were grouped according to theme. In some cases, respondents provided more than one 
answer.  
The last column always shows the total number of people (unweighted) from each demographic 
subgroup that responded to this particular question. Some questions apply only to certain types 
of residents based on their answers to other questions in the survey. Data was suppressed in 
cases where the row total is less than 20 to protect respondent confidentiality.  
                                                 
1  
The survey also had options for respondents to identify as ‘transgender’ or ‘another way,’ but the number of responses for 
these options was too small to report.
